I guess what brings me in is… it’s this reaction I’m having at work, mostly around the stockroom. It’s mostly bats, yeah. Or… I guess anything that my brain reads as a bat for even half a second. Like if I’m in the back hallway and something flutters quickly, or I catch a dark shape out of the corner of my eye, my mind just jumps straight to “bat,” and then it’s like I’m in danger right then.

The frustrating thing is I do know, logically, that a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me. I can say that sitting here. But in the moment, it doesn’t feel like that matters. If it looks like one, or sounds like one, or even moves in that quick flapping way, my body and my head react like it’s an actual threat.

I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I mostly notice it at work, since that’s where I’m around the stockroom and the back areas more. That’s the place my mind goes to right away when I think about it.

I mean, I don’t have a lot of examples outside of work that are as clear. It’s not like I’m walking around all day everywhere expecting bats, if that makes sense. But I also don’t know if that’s because it really only happens at work, or because I’m not usually in places where I’d think there could be one. Like at home I’m not usually dealing with dark storage rooms or back hallways, so it just doesn’t come up in the same way.

So… I guess the honest answer is, I’ve mainly experienced it at work, and I haven’t really noticed it much in other places.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what brings me in is… it’s this reaction I’m having at work, mostly around the stockroom. If I even think there might be a bat in there, or something flapping around, my body just reacts right away. Like, before I can even talk myself through it, my heart kind of jumps, I start sweating, and I get this really strong feeling that I need to get out of there immediately.\n\nIt’s not just “oh, that’s unpleasant” — it feels like my whole body is telling me to leave right then. And I know it probably sounds kind of specific, but it’s been bothering me enough that I figured I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically bats that trigger this reaction, or would other animals or situations cause a similar response?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly bats, yeah. I can say that sitting here. But in the moment, it doesn’t feel like that matters. That’s the place my mind goes to right away when I think about it.\n\nI mean, I don’t have a lot of examples outside of work that are as clear. It’s not like I’m walking around all day everywhere expecting bats, if that makes sense. Like at home I’m not usually dealing with dark storage rooms or back hallways, so it just doesn’t come up in the same way.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is, I’ve mainly experienced it at work, and I haven’t really noticed it much in other places.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this reaction to bats or things that resemble bats?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two years now, I think. Um… I don’t think so? Not in the same way, at least. I mean, everybody gets stressed, right—work can be annoying, money stuff, customers being difficult, normal life things—but I don’t walk around generally feeling on edge for no reason, if that’s what you mean.

I guess sometimes after something reminds me of it, I might stay a little keyed up for a while, but I wouldn’t say I’m anxious all the time or in situations that have nothing to do with bats. It’s more like my brain needs some kind of trigger to go there.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do. More than I probably want to admit, honestly.

At work especially, I’ve been swapping out closing shifts when I can, because closing is when I end up around the back areas more, and it’s darker and quieter and my mind starts going there. I don’t always say exactly why, either — I’ll just try to trade with somebody or make it sound like a scheduling thing, because it feels embarrassing to say, “I don’t want to be back there because I’m scared there might be a bat.”

And if I have to go near the loading area, I’ll take the long way around instead of going the normal way, even if it’s kind of ridiculous and takes extra time. I know that sounds like a small thing, but I’ll actually plan my route through the store around avoiding that part if I can.

Then there are times I can’t avoid it, like if I have to check the stairwell or something. I’ll still do it, but I make myself do it really fast. I’ll have my phone in my hand, like having the flashlight ready or just… I don’t know, like it makes me feel less trapped. But I’m scared the whole time, and I’m basically trying to get in and out as quickly as possible.

That’s the place my mind goes to right away when I think about it.\n\nI mean, I don’t have a lot of examples outside of work that are as clear. It’s not like I’m walking around all day everywhere expecting bats, if that makes sense. If I’m just at home watching TV or doing errands or something, I’m not usually tense like that. At least I haven’t really noticed it.\n\nI guess sometimes after something reminds me of it, I might stay a little keyed up for a while, but I wouldn’t say I’m anxious all the time or in situations that have nothing to do with bats. It’s more like my brain needs some kind of trigger to go there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or activities because you're afraid of encountering a bat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I probably want to admit, honestly.\n\nAt work especially, I’ve been swapping out closing shifts when I can, because closing is when I end up around the back areas more, and it’s darker and quieter and my mind starts going there. I don’t always say exactly why, either — I’ll just try to trade with somebody or make it sound like a scheduling thing, because it feels embarrassing to say, “I don’t want to be back there because I’m scared there might be a bat.”\n\nAnd if I have to go near the loading area, I’ll take the long way around instead of going the normal way, even if it’s kind of ridiculous and takes extra time. I know that sounds like a small thing, but I’ll actually plan my route through the store around avoiding that part if I can.\n\nThen there are times I can’t avoid it, like if I have to check the stairwell or something. I’ll still do it, but I make myself do it really fast. I’ll have my phone in my hand, like having the flashlight ready or just… I don’t know, like it makes me feel less trapped. But I’m scared the whole time, and I’m basically trying to get in and out as quickly as possible.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas avoiding places where you might encounter bats started to affect your personal life or relationships outside of work?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, outside of work, maybe not so much with friends or dating or anything like that, but it’s definitely affecting my life in a bigger way because of work. Work is kind of where everything is tied together for me right now.

My manager has started noticing that I keep trying to trade out of closing duties, and I think at first he maybe thought it was just normal schedule stuff, but now it’s happened enough that he’s questioning it. And the closing shift is a big deal because if you’re going to be a shift lead, you have to be someone they can count on after hours — like doing the checks, making sure the back areas are handled, that sort of thing.

And that’s the part that really bothers me, because I’ve been trying to work toward a more permanent management position. It’s not like I’m just avoiding some little inconvenience. This could actually cost me that opportunity if they decide I’m not dependable enough.

My manager hasn’t flat-out said, “You’re not getting it,” but I can tell he’s wondering if I can handle it. He’s asked why I’m always switching those shifts, and I don’t really know how to explain it without sounding ridiculous. So I give these vague answers, which probably makes me look worse.

So, yeah… it’s not just embarrassing anymore. It feels like this thing could mess up something I’ve been working toward.

I can say that sitting here. But in the moment, it doesn’t feel like that matters. I’ll still do it, but I make myself do it really fast. I’ll have my phone in my hand, like having the flashlight ready or just… I don’t know, like it makes me feel less trapped. But I’m scared the whole time, and I’m basically trying to get in and out as quickly as possible.\n\nDoctor: Has avoiding places where you might encounter bats started to affect your personal life or relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, outside of work, maybe not so much with friends or dating or anything like that, but it’s definitely affecting my life in a bigger way because of work. Work is kind of where everything is tied together for me right now.\n\nMy manager has started noticing that I keep trying to trade out of closing duties, and I think at first he maybe thought it was just normal schedule stuff, but now it’s happened enough that he’s questioning it. And the closing shift is a big deal because if you’re going to be a shift lead, you have to be someone they can count on after hours — like doing the checks, making sure the back areas are handled, that sort of thing.\n\nAnd that’s the part that really bothers me, because I’ve been trying to work toward a more permanent management position. It’s not like I’m just avoiding some little inconvenience. This could actually cost me that opportunity if they decide I’m not dependable enough.\n\nMy manager hasn’t flat-out said, “You’re not getting it,” but I can tell he’s wondering if I can handle it. He’s asked why I’m always switching those shifts, and I don’t really know how to explain it without sounding ridiculous. So I give these vague answers, which probably makes me look worse.\n\nSo, yeah… it’s not just embarrassing anymore. It feels like this thing could mess up something I’ve been working toward.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there any situations outside of work where you feel like your fear of bats is holding you back or causing you significant distress?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Outside of work… I’m trying to think. Not really in a big way, I don’t think. I mean, I don’t love being outside at night if something makes me think about bats, like if it’s really dark or there are trees overhead or something, but I can’t say I’ve stopped seeing people or stopped doing normal life stuff because of it.

Most of the real problem is tied to work, because that’s where I feel like I might actually have to go into those back areas or deal with it if one showed up. Outside work, I can usually just… not put myself in that situation, I guess, or it doesn’t come up as much.

So I wouldn’t say it’s causing major problems in my personal life, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more like an uncomfortable thought here and there, unless something specifically reminds me of it.

You are about\nto talk with the doctor.\n\nLooking at the dialogue so far and the doctor's last question, work out what it is asking\nabout, and pick the item of your symptom profile to answer it with.\n\n[Symptom Profiles]\n- key: S062\n  name: Specific_Phobic_Fear\n  description: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).\n  manifestation: I know a bat in the store probably isn't going to hurt me, but if I see anything that looks like one or even a quick flutter in the back hallway, my mind treats it like I'm in real danger.\n- key: S063\n  name: Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response\n  description: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.\n  manifestation: What brings me in is that the second I think there might be a bat or something flapping around the stockroom, my heart jumps, I start sweating, and I feel like I need to get out right then.\n- key: S064\n  name: Phobic_Avoidance_Endure\n  description: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.\n  manifestation: I've been swapping out closing shifts, taking the long way around the loading area, and when I have to check the stairwell I make myself do it fast while holding my phone and feeling scared the whole time.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: My manager has started questioning whether I can handle the shift lead role because I keep trading closing duties and I'm not as dependable for the after-hours checks. It feels like this could cost me the permanent management position I was working toward.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 0 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about two years, since that night a bat got into the back corridor during a receiving shift.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 37 / retail_salesperson / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S063\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or activities because you're afraid of encountering a bat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I probably want to admit, honestly.\n\nAt work especially, I’ve been swapping out closing shifts when I can, because closing is when I end up around the back areas more, and it’s darker and quieter and my mind starts going there. I don’t always say exactly why, either — I’ll just try to trade with somebody or make it sound like a scheduling thing, because it feels embarrassing to say, “I don’t want to be back there because I’m scared there might be a bat.”\n\nAnd if I have to go near the loading area, I’ll take the long way around instead of going the normal way, even if it’s kind of ridiculous and takes extra time. I know that sounds like a small thing, but I’ll actually plan my route through the store around avoiding that part if I can.\n\nThen there are times I can’t avoid it, like if I have to check the stairwell or something. I’ll still do it, but I make myself do it really fast. I’ll have my phone in my hand, like having the flashlight ready or just… I don’t know, like it makes me feel less trapped. But I’m scared the whole time, and I’m basically trying to get in and out as quickly as possible.\n\nDoctor: Has avoiding places where you might encounter bats started to affect your personal life or relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, outside of work, maybe not so much with friends or dating or anything like that, but it’s definitely affecting my life in a bigger way because of work. Work is kind of where everything is tied together for me right now.\n\nMy manager has started noticing that I keep trying to trade out of closing duties, and I think at first he maybe thought it was just normal schedule stuff, but now it’s happened enough that he’s questioning it. And the closing shift is a big deal because if you’re going to be a shift lead, you have to be someone they can count on after hours — like doing the checks, making sure the back areas are handled, that sort of thing.\n\nAnd that’s the part that really bothers me, because I’ve been trying to work toward a more permanent management position. It’s not like I’m just avoiding some little inconvenience. This could actually cost me that opportunity if they decide I’m not dependable enough.\n\nMy manager hasn’t flat-out said, “You’re not getting it,” but I can tell he’s wondering if I can handle it. He’s asked why I’m always switching those shifts, and I don’t really know how to explain it without sounding ridiculous. So I give these vague answers, which probably makes me look worse.\n\nSo, yeah… it’s not just embarrassing anymore. It feels like this thing could mess up something I’ve been working toward.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations outside of work where you feel like your fear of bats is holding you back or causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I’m trying to think. Not really in a big way, I don’t think. I mean, I don’t love being outside at night if something makes me think about bats, like if it’s really dark or there are trees overhead or something, but I can’t say I’ve stopped seeing people or stopped doing normal life stuff because of it.\n\nMost of the real problem is tied to work, because that’s where I feel like I might actually have to go into those back areas or deal with it if one showed up. Outside work, I can usually just… not put myself in that situation, I guess, or it doesn’t come up as much.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s causing major problems in my personal life, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more like an uncomfortable thought here and there, unless something specifically reminds me of it.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre you avoiding any social activities or hobbies outside of work because of your fear of bats?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really social activities or hobbies, no… at least I don’t think so. I mean, I’m not skipping parties or avoiding friends because of bats or anything like that.

It’s more that the avoiding shows up at work. Like I said, I’ve been swapping out closing shifts when I can, because those are the times I feel like I’m more likely to have to go through the darker back areas. And I’ll take the long way around the loading area instead of just walking through it normally, even if it makes no sense time-wise. If I have to check the stairwell, I’ll do it, but I kind of rush through it with my phone in my hand, like I’m ready to turn the flashlight on or call someone, and I’m scared the whole time.

So outside of work, no, not in a major way. But at work it’s definitely making me avoid things or get through them as fast as possible.

And I know it probably sounds kind of specific, but it’s been bothering me enough that I figured I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically bats that trigger this reaction, or would other animals or situations cause a similar response?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly bats, yeah. Or… I guess anything that my brain reads as a bat for even half a second. I can say that sitting here. But in the moment, it doesn’t feel like that matters. If it looks like one, or sounds like one, or even moves in that quick flapping way, my body and my head react like it’s an actual threat.\n\nDoctor: Does this reaction to bats or things that resemble bats happen only at work, or have you experienced it in other places as well?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I mostly notice it at work, since that’s where I’m around the stockroom and the back areas more. That’s the place my mind goes to right away when I think about it.\n\nI mean, I don’t have a lot of examples outside of work that are as clear. It’s not like I’m walking around all day everywhere expecting bats, if that makes sense. But I also don’t know if that’s because it really only happens at work, or because I’m not usually in places where I’d think there could be one. Like at home I’m not usually dealing with dark storage rooms or back hallways, so it just doesn’t come up in the same way.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is, I’ve mainly experienced it at work, and I haven’t really noticed it much in other places.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this reaction to bats or things that resemble bats?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two years now, I think. It started after this one night at work when I was on a receiving shift, and a bat somehow got into the back corridor.\n\nBefore that, I mean, I don’t remember reacting this strongly. I probably wouldn’t have liked seeing one, but it wasn’t like this. That night kind of sticks in my head, because it was back in that corridor area and it was moving around, and ever since then, anything that reminds me of that has set me off. Not in the same way, at least. I mean, everybody gets stressed, right—work can be annoying, money stuff, customers being difficult, normal life things—but I don’t walk around generally feeling on edge for no reason, if that’s what you mean.\n\nThe anxious feeling I’m talking about is pretty tied to the bat thing, or places where I start thinking there could be one. If I’m just at home watching TV or doing errands or something, I’m not usually tense like that. At least I haven’t really noticed it.\n\nI guess sometimes after something reminds me of it, I might stay a little keyed up for a while, but I wouldn’t say I’m anxious all the time or in situations that have nothing to do with bats. Or… I guess anything that my brain reads as a bat for even half a second. Like if I’m in the back hallway and something flutters quickly, or I catch a dark shape out of the corner of my eye, my mind just jumps straight to “bat,” and then it’s like I’m in danger right then.\n\nThe frustrating thing is I do know, logically, that a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me. I can say that sitting here. But in the moment, it doesn’t feel like that matters. If it looks like one, or sounds like one, or even moves in that quick flapping way, my body and my head react like it’s an actual threat.\n\nDoctor: Does this reaction to bats or things that resemble bats happen only at work, or have you experienced it in other places as well?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I mostly notice it at work, since that’s where I’m around the stockroom and the back areas more. That’s the place my mind goes to right away when I think about it.\n\nI mean, I don’t have a lot of examples outside of work that are as clear. It’s not like I’m walking around all day everywhere expecting bats, if that makes sense. But I also don’t know if that’s because it really only happens at work, or because I’m not usually in places where I’d think there could be one. Like at home I’m not usually dealing with dark storage rooms or back hallways, so it just doesn’t come up in the same way.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is, I’ve mainly experienced it at work, and I haven’t really noticed it much in other places.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this reaction to bats or things that resemble bats?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two years now, I think. It started after this one night at work when I was on a receiving shift, and a bat somehow got into the back corridor.\n\nBefore that, I mean, I don’t remember reacting this strongly. I probably wouldn’t have liked seeing one, but it wasn’t like this. That night kind of sticks in my head, because it was back in that corridor area and it was moving around, and ever since then, anything that reminds me of that has set me off. So… yeah, roughly two years.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or on edge in situations that have nothing to do with bats?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in the same way, at least. I mean, everybody gets stressed, right—work can be annoying, money stuff, customers being difficult, normal life things—but I don’t walk around generally feeling on edge for no reason, if that’s what you mean.\n\nThe anxious feeling I’m talking about is pretty tied to the bat thing, or places where I start thinking there could be one. If I’m just at home watching TV or doing errands or something, I’m not usually tense like that. At least I haven’t really noticed it.\n\nI guess sometimes after something reminds me of it, I might stay a little keyed up for a while, but I wouldn’t say I’m anxious all the time or in situations that have nothing to do with bats. It’s more like my brain needs some kind of trigger to go there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or activities because you're afraid of encountering a bat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I probably want to admit, honestly.\n\nAt work especially, I’ve been swapping out closing shifts when I can, because closing is when I end up around the back areas more, and it’s darker and quieter and my mind starts going there. I don’t always say exactly why, either — I’ll just try to trade with somebody or make it sound like a scheduling thing, because it feels embarrassing to say, “I don’t want to be back there because I’m scared there might be a bat.”\n\nAnd if I have to go near the loading area, I’ll take the long way around instead of going the normal way, even if it’s kind of ridiculous and takes extra time. I know that sounds like a small thing, but I’ll actually plan my route through the store around avoiding that part if I can.\n\nThen there are times I can’t avoid it, like if I have to check the stairwell or something. I’ll still do it, but I make myself do it really fast. I’ll have my phone in my hand, like having the flashlight ready or just… I don’t know, like it makes me feel less trapped. But I’m scared the whole time, and I’m basically trying to get in and out as quickly as possible.\n\nDoctor: Has avoiding places where you might encounter bats started to affect your personal life or relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, outside of work, maybe not so much with friends or dating or anything like that, but it’s definitely affecting my life in a bigger way because of work. Work is kind of where everything is tied together for me right now.\n\nMy manager has started noticing that I keep trying to trade out of closing duties, and I think at first he maybe thought it was just normal schedule stuff, but now it’s happened enough that he’s questioning it. And the closing shift is a big deal because if you’re going to be a shift lead, you have to be someone they can count on after hours — like doing the checks, making sure the back areas are handled, that sort of thing.\n\nAnd that’s the part that really bothers me, because I’ve been trying to work toward a more permanent management position. It’s not like I’m just avoiding some little inconvenience. This could actually cost me that opportunity if they decide I’m not dependable enough.\n\nMy manager hasn’t flat-out said, “You’re not getting it,” but I can tell he’s wondering if I can handle it. He’s asked why I’m always switching those shifts, and I don’t really know how to explain it without sounding ridiculous. So I give these vague answers, which probably makes me look worse.\n\nSo, yeah… it’s not just embarrassing anymore. It feels like this thing could mess up something I’ve been working toward.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations outside of work where you feel like your fear of bats is holding you back or causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I’m trying to think. Not really in a big way, I don’t think. I mean, I don’t love being outside at night if something makes me think about bats, like if it’s really dark or there are trees overhead or something, but I can’t say I’ve stopped seeing people or stopped doing normal life stuff because of it.\n\nMost of the real problem is tied to work, because that’s where I feel like I might actually have to go into those back areas or deal with it if one showed up. Outside work, I can usually just… not put myself in that situation, I guess, or it doesn’t come up as much.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s causing major problems in my personal life, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more like an uncomfortable thought here and there, unless something specifically reminds me of it.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any social activities or hobbies outside of work because of your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: Not really social activities or hobbies, no… at least I don’t think so. I mean, I’m not skipping parties or avoiding friends because of bats or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more that the avoiding shows up at work. Like I said, I’ve been swapping out closing shifts when I can, because those are the times I feel like I’m more likely to have to go through the darker back areas. And I’ll take the long way around the loading area instead of just walking through it normally, even if it makes no sense time-wise. If I have to check the stairwell, I’ll do it, but I kind of rush through it with my phone in my hand, like I’m ready to turn the flashlight on or call someone, and I’m scared the whole time.\n\nSo outside of work, no, not in a major way. But at work it’s definitely making me avoid things or get through them as fast as possible.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your fear of bats is affecting your ability to enjoy or participate in daily activities outside of your job?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any clear way. Like, day-to-day outside of work, I’m still doing the normal things I need to do. I go to the store, I see people, I can go out to eat or whatever. I’m not sitting at home refusing to leave because of it.\n\nI mean, if I’m outside at night and something makes me think about bats — like if it’s quiet and dark, or I’m walking near trees — then I might feel uncomfortable, and I’ll probably look around more than I need to. But I wouldn’t say it’s stopping me from enjoying my life outside of work in a major way.\n\nIt’s really more that work puts me in situations where I feel like I can’t just avoid it without consequences. Outside of work, it’s more like… if I don’t want to walk through some dark area, I just don’t, and nobody really notices or cares. So I haven’t noticed it affecting my daily activities outside my job too much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t know that I’ve thought about it that way, honestly. Like… “spread” to other things? I guess I worry sometimes that if I keep avoiding the bat-related stuff at work, maybe I’ll get more and more nervous about those areas in general, even if there isn’t actually a bat there. It feels like this thing could mess up something I’ve been working toward.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations outside of work where you feel like your fear of bats is holding you back or causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I’m trying to think. Not really in a big way, I don’t think. Like, day-to-day outside of work, I’m still doing the normal things I need to do. I go to the store, I see people, I can go out to eat or whatever. I’m not sitting at home refusing to leave because of it.\n\nI mean, if I’m outside at night and something makes me think about bats — like if it’s quiet and dark, or I’m walking near trees — then I might feel uncomfortable, and I’ll probably look around more than I need to. Outside of work, it’s more like… if I don’t want to walk through some dark area, I just don’t, and nobody really notices or cares. At work it feels more obvious because there are certain areas I have to deal with, but with family or friends, I guess it just doesn’t come up that much. So… no, I wouldn’t say it’s really affected my relationships.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what brings me in is… it’s this reaction I’m having at work, mostly around the stockroom. If I even think there might be a bat in there, or something flapping around, my body just reacts right away. Like, before I can even talk myself through it, my heart kind of jumps, I start sweating, and I get this really strong feeling that I need to get out of there immediately.\n\nIt’s not just “oh, that’s unpleasant” — it feels like my whole body is telling me to leave right then. And I know it probably sounds kind of specific, but it’s been bothering me enough that I figured I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically bats that trigger this reaction, or would other animals or situations cause a similar response?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly bats, yeah. Or… I guess anything that my brain reads as a bat for even half a second. Like if I’m in the back hallway and something flutters quickly, or I catch a dark shape out of the corner of my eye, my mind just jumps straight to “bat,” and then it’s like I’m in danger right then.\n\nThe frustrating thing is I do know, logically, that a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me. I can say that sitting here. But in the moment, it doesn’t feel like that matters. If it looks like one, or sounds like one, or even moves in that quick flapping way, my body and my head react like it’s an actual threat.\n\nDoctor: Does this reaction to bats or things that resemble bats happen only at work, or have you experienced it in other places as well?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I mostly notice it at work, since that’s where I’m around the stockroom and the back areas more. That’s the place my mind goes to right away when I think about it.\n\nI mean, I don’t have a lot of examples outside of work that are as clear. It’s not like I’m walking around all day everywhere expecting bats, if that makes sense. But I also don’t know if that’s because it really only happens at work, or because I’m not usually in places where I’d think there could be one. Like at home I’m not usually dealing with dark storage rooms or back hallways, so it just doesn’t come up in the same way.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is, I’ve mainly experienced it at work, and I haven’t really noticed it much in other places.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this reaction to bats or things that resemble bats?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two years now, I think. It started after this one night at work when I was on a receiving shift, and a bat somehow got into the back corridor.\n\nBefore that, I mean, I don’t remember reacting this strongly. I probably wouldn’t have liked seeing one, but it wasn’t like this. That night kind of sticks in my head, because it was back in that corridor area and it was moving around, and ever since then, anything that reminds me of that has set me off. So… yeah, roughly two years.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or on edge in situations that have nothing to do with bats?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in the same way, at least. I mean, everybody gets stressed, right—work can be annoying, money stuff, customers being difficult, normal life things—but I don’t walk around generally feeling on edge for no reason, if that’s what you mean.\n\nThe anxious feeling I’m talking about is pretty tied to the bat thing, or places where I start thinking there could be one. If I’m just at home watching TV or doing errands or something, I’m not usually tense like that. At least I haven’t really noticed it.\n\nI guess sometimes after something reminds me of it, I might stay a little keyed up for a while, but I wouldn’t say I’m anxious all the time or in situations that have nothing to do with bats. It’s more like my brain needs some kind of trigger to go there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or activities because you're afraid of encountering a bat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I probably want to admit, honestly.\n\nAt work especially, I’ve been swapping out closing shifts when I can, because closing is when I end up around the back areas more, and it’s darker and quieter and my mind starts going there. I don’t always say exactly why, either — I’ll just try to trade with somebody or make it sound like a scheduling thing, because it feels embarrassing to say, “I don’t want to be back there because I’m scared there might be a bat.”\n\nAnd if I have to go near the loading area, I’ll take the long way around instead of going the normal way, even if it’s kind of ridiculous and takes extra time. I know that sounds like a small thing, but I’ll actually plan my route through the store around avoiding that part if I can.\n\nThen there are times I can’t avoid it, like if I have to check the stairwell or something. I’ll still do it, but I make myself do it really fast. I’ll have my phone in my hand, like having the flashlight ready or just… I don’t know, like it makes me feel less trapped. But I’m scared the whole time, and I’m basically trying to get in and out as quickly as possible.\n\nDoctor: Has avoiding places where you might encounter bats started to affect your personal life or relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, outside of work, maybe not so much with friends or dating or anything like that, but it’s definitely affecting my life in a bigger way because of work. Work is kind of where everything is tied together for me right now.\n\nMy manager has started noticing that I keep trying to trade out of closing duties, and I think at first he maybe thought it was just normal schedule stuff, but now it’s happened enough that he’s questioning it. And the closing shift is a big deal because if you’re going to be a shift lead, you have to be someone they can count on after hours — like doing the checks, making sure the back areas are handled, that sort of thing.\n\nAnd that’s the part that really bothers me, because I’ve been trying to work toward a more permanent management position. It’s not like I’m just avoiding some little inconvenience. This could actually cost me that opportunity if they decide I’m not dependable enough.\n\nMy manager hasn’t flat-out said, “You’re not getting it,” but I can tell he’s wondering if I can handle it. He’s asked why I’m always switching those shifts, and I don’t really know how to explain it without sounding ridiculous. So I give these vague answers, which probably makes me look worse.\n\nSo, yeah… it’s not just embarrassing anymore. It feels like this thing could mess up something I’ve been working toward.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations outside of work where you feel like your fear of bats is holding you back or causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I’m trying to think. Not really in a big way, I don’t think. I mean, I don’t love being outside at night if something makes me think about bats, like if it’s really dark or there are trees overhead or something, but I can’t say I’ve stopped seeing people or stopped doing normal life stuff because of it.\n\nMost of the real problem is tied to work, because that’s where I feel like I might actually have to go into those back areas or deal with it if one showed up. Outside work, I can usually just… not put myself in that situation, I guess, or it doesn’t come up as much.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s causing major problems in my personal life, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more like an uncomfortable thought here and there, unless something specifically reminds me of it.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any social activities or hobbies outside of work because of your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: Not really social activities or hobbies, no… at least I don’t think so. I mean, I’m not skipping parties or avoiding friends because of bats or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more that the avoiding shows up at work. Like I said, I’ve been swapping out closing shifts when I can, because those are the times I feel like I’m more likely to have to go through the darker back areas. And I’ll take the long way around the loading area instead of just walking through it normally, even if it makes no sense time-wise. If I have to check the stairwell, I’ll do it, but I kind of rush through it with my phone in my hand, like I’m ready to turn the flashlight on or call someone, and I’m scared the whole time.\n\nSo outside of work, no, not in a major way. But at work it’s definitely making me avoid things or get through them as fast as possible.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your fear of bats is affecting your ability to enjoy or participate in daily activities outside of your job?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any clear way. Like, day-to-day outside of work, I’m still doing the normal things I need to do. I go to the store, I see people, I can go out to eat or whatever. I’m not sitting at home refusing to leave because of it.\n\nI mean, if I’m outside at night and something makes me think about bats — like if it’s quiet and dark, or I’m walking near trees — then I might feel uncomfortable, and I’ll probably look around more than I need to. But I wouldn’t say it’s stopping me from enjoying my life outside of work in a major way.\n\nIt’s really more that work puts me in situations where I feel like I can’t just avoid it without consequences. Outside of work, it’s more like… if I don’t want to walk through some dark area, I just don’t, and nobody really notices or cares. So I haven’t noticed it affecting my daily activities outside my job too much.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried that your fear of bats might spread to other things or situations, making it harder for you to manage your daily life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve thought about it that way, honestly. Like… “spread” to other things? I guess I worry sometimes that if I keep avoiding the bat-related stuff at work, maybe I’ll get more and more nervous about those areas in general, even if there isn’t actually a bat there. But I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nI haven’t really noticed it turning into a fear of, like, all animals or being outside in general or anything like that. But I don’t know if I’m afraid of the place itself, or if my brain is just going, “That’s where a bat could be.”\n\nSo… I guess I’m a little worried it could get harder to manage if I let it keep controlling what I do, but I can’t say it has spread in a big way so far. I mean, my family and friends know I don’t like bats, or at least I think they’ve picked up on it, but it hasn’t turned into arguments or anything like that.\n\nI’m not avoiding seeing them because of it, and I’m not asking people to change plans around me, at least not in any big way. If someone wanted to do something outdoors at night, I might feel a little tense depending on where it was, but I don’t think it’s gotten to the point where it’s affecting the relationship itself.\n\nAt work it feels more obvious because there are certain areas I have to deal with, but with family or friends, I guess it just doesn’t come up that much. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, not my whole daily routine, but there are definitely parts of it I’ve adjusted, mostly around work.

The biggest thing is closing shifts. I’ve been trying to swap out of those when I can, or trade with somebody else, because that’s when I get more nervous about running into bats, especially around the back areas or when it’s darker. I don’t always say the full reason, honestly. Sometimes I’ll just say I have something going on or ask if someone wants to switch, but in my head it’s really because I don’t want to be there at that time.

And then at work I’ll take the long way around the loading area instead of going through it directly. It’s kind of embarrassing because it adds time and it probably looks unnecessary, but I just feel better not going through that spot if I can avoid it. If I have to pass by it, I’m kind of tense the whole time.

The stairwell is another one. If I have to check it, I’ll still do it, but I do it really fast. I’ll hold my phone in my hand, like that somehow makes me feel more prepared, and I’ll kind of shine the light or keep it ready. I know it probably looks ridiculous, but I’m scared the whole time, and I’m just trying to get in and out as quickly as possible.

So, yeah, I guess I have changed some things. It’s not like every minute of my day is different, but I’m definitely planning around it more than I’d like to admit.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what brings me in is… it’s this reaction I’m having at work, mostly around the stockroom. If I even think there might be a bat in there, or something flapping around, my body just reacts right away. Like, before I can even talk myself through it, my heart kind of jumps, I start sweating, and I get this really strong feeling that I need to get out of there immediately.\n\nIt’s not just “oh, that’s unpleasant” — it feels like my whole body is telling me to leave right then. And I know it probably sounds kind of specific, but it’s been bothering me enough that I figured I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically bats that trigger this reaction, or would other animals or situations cause a similar response?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly bats, yeah. Or… I guess anything that my brain reads as a bat for even half a second. Like if I’m in the back hallway and something flutters quickly, or I catch a dark shape out of the corner of my eye, my mind just jumps straight to “bat,” and then it’s like I’m in danger right then.\n\nThe frustrating thing is I do know, logically, that a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me. I can say that sitting here. But in the moment, it doesn’t feel like that matters. If it looks like one, or sounds like one, or even moves in that quick flapping way, my body and my head react like it’s an actual threat.\n\nDoctor: Does this reaction to bats or things that resemble bats happen only at work, or have you experienced it in other places as well?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I mostly notice it at work, since that’s where I’m around the stockroom and the back areas more. That’s the place my mind goes to right away when I think about it.\n\nI mean, I don’t have a lot of examples outside of work that are as clear. It’s not like I’m walking around all day everywhere expecting bats, if that makes sense. But I also don’t know if that’s because it really only happens at work, or because I’m not usually in places where I’d think there could be one. Like at home I’m not usually dealing with dark storage rooms or back hallways, so it just doesn’t come up in the same way.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is, I’ve mainly experienced it at work, and I haven’t really noticed it much in other places.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this reaction to bats or things that resemble bats?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two years now, I think. It started after this one night at work when I was on a receiving shift, and a bat somehow got into the back corridor.\n\nBefore that, I mean, I don’t remember reacting this strongly. I probably wouldn’t have liked seeing one, but it wasn’t like this. That night kind of sticks in my head, because it was back in that corridor area and it was moving around, and ever since then, anything that reminds me of that has set me off. So… yeah, roughly two years.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or on edge in situations that have nothing to do with bats?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in the same way, at least. I mean, everybody gets stressed, right—work can be annoying, money stuff, customers being difficult, normal life things—but I don’t walk around generally feeling on edge for no reason, if that’s what you mean.\n\nThe anxious feeling I’m talking about is pretty tied to the bat thing, or places where I start thinking there could be one. If I’m just at home watching TV or doing errands or something, I’m not usually tense like that. At least I haven’t really noticed it.\n\nI guess sometimes after something reminds me of it, I might stay a little keyed up for a while, but I wouldn’t say I’m anxious all the time or in situations that have nothing to do with bats. It’s more like my brain needs some kind of trigger to go there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or activities because you're afraid of encountering a bat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I probably want to admit, honestly.\n\nAt work especially, I’ve been swapping out closing shifts when I can, because closing is when I end up around the back areas more, and it’s darker and quieter and my mind starts going there. I don’t always say exactly why, either — I’ll just try to trade with somebody or make it sound like a scheduling thing, because it feels embarrassing to say, “I don’t want to be back there because I’m scared there might be a bat.”\n\nAnd if I have to go near the loading area, I’ll take the long way around instead of going the normal way, even if it’s kind of ridiculous and takes extra time. I know that sounds like a small thing, but I’ll actually plan my route through the store around avoiding that part if I can.\n\nThen there are times I can’t avoid it, like if I have to check the stairwell or something. I’ll still do it, but I make myself do it really fast. I’ll have my phone in my hand, like having the flashlight ready or just… I don’t know, like it makes me feel less trapped. But I’m scared the whole time, and I’m basically trying to get in and out as quickly as possible.\n\nDoctor: Has avoiding places where you might encounter bats started to affect your personal life or relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, outside of work, maybe not so much with friends or dating or anything like that, but it’s definitely affecting my life in a bigger way because of work. Work is kind of where everything is tied together for me right now.\n\nMy manager has started noticing that I keep trying to trade out of closing duties, and I think at first he maybe thought it was just normal schedule stuff, but now it’s happened enough that he’s questioning it. And the closing shift is a big deal because if you’re going to be a shift lead, you have to be someone they can count on after hours — like doing the checks, making sure the back areas are handled, that sort of thing.\n\nAnd that’s the part that really bothers me, because I’ve been trying to work toward a more permanent management position. It’s not like I’m just avoiding some little inconvenience. This could actually cost me that opportunity if they decide I’m not dependable enough.\n\nMy manager hasn’t flat-out said, “You’re not getting it,” but I can tell he’s wondering if I can handle it. He’s asked why I’m always switching those shifts, and I don’t really know how to explain it without sounding ridiculous. So I give these vague answers, which probably makes me look worse.\n\nSo, yeah… it’s not just embarrassing anymore. It feels like this thing could mess up something I’ve been working toward.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations outside of work where you feel like your fear of bats is holding you back or causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I’m trying to think. Not really in a big way, I don’t think. I mean, I don’t love being outside at night if something makes me think about bats, like if it’s really dark or there are trees overhead or something, but I can’t say I’ve stopped seeing people or stopped doing normal life stuff because of it.\n\nMost of the real problem is tied to work, because that’s where I feel like I might actually have to go into those back areas or deal with it if one showed up. Outside work, I can usually just… not put myself in that situation, I guess, or it doesn’t come up as much.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s causing major problems in my personal life, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more like an uncomfortable thought here and there, unless something specifically reminds me of it.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any social activities or hobbies outside of work because of your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: Not really social activities or hobbies, no… at least I don’t think so. I mean, I’m not skipping parties or avoiding friends because of bats or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more that the avoiding shows up at work. Like I said, I’ve been swapping out closing shifts when I can, because those are the times I feel like I’m more likely to have to go through the darker back areas. And I’ll take the long way around the loading area instead of just walking through it normally, even if it makes no sense time-wise. If I have to check the stairwell, I’ll do it, but I kind of rush through it with my phone in my hand, like I’m ready to turn the flashlight on or call someone, and I’m scared the whole time.\n\nSo outside of work, no, not in a major way. But at work it’s definitely making me avoid things or get through them as fast as possible.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your fear of bats is affecting your ability to enjoy or participate in daily activities outside of your job?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any clear way. Like, day-to-day outside of work, I’m still doing the normal things I need to do. I go to the store, I see people, I can go out to eat or whatever. I’m not sitting at home refusing to leave because of it.\n\nI mean, if I’m outside at night and something makes me think about bats — like if it’s quiet and dark, or I’m walking near trees — then I might feel uncomfortable, and I’ll probably look around more than I need to. But I wouldn’t say it’s stopping me from enjoying my life outside of work in a major way.\n\nIt’s really more that work puts me in situations where I feel like I can’t just avoid it without consequences. Outside of work, it’s more like… if I don’t want to walk through some dark area, I just don’t, and nobody really notices or cares. So I haven’t noticed it affecting my daily activities outside my job too much.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried that your fear of bats might spread to other things or situations, making it harder for you to manage your daily life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve thought about it that way, honestly. This could actually cost me that opportunity if they decide I’m not dependable enough.\n\nMy manager hasn’t flat-out said, “You’re not getting it,” but I can tell he’s wondering if I can handle it. He’s asked why I’m always switching those shifts, and I don’t really know how to explain it without sounding ridiculous. So I give these vague answers, which probably makes me look worse.\n\nSo, yeah… it’s not just embarrassing anymore. It feels like this thing could mess up something I’ve been working toward.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations outside of work where you feel like your fear of bats is holding you back or causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I’m trying to think. Not really in a big way, I don’t think. I mean, I don’t love being outside at night if something makes me think about bats, like if it’s really dark or there are trees overhead or something, but I can’t say I’ve stopped seeing people or stopped doing normal life stuff because of it.\n\nMost of the real problem is tied to work, because that’s where I feel like I might actually have to go into those back areas or deal with it if one showed up. Outside work, I can usually just… not put myself in that situation, I guess, or it doesn’t come up as much.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s causing major problems in my personal life, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more like an uncomfortable thought here and there, unless something specifically reminds me of it.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any social activities or hobbies outside of work because of your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: Not really social activities or hobbies, no… at least I don’t think so. I mean, I’m not skipping parties or avoiding friends because of bats or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more that the avoiding shows up at work. Like I said, I’ve been swapping out closing shifts when I can, because those are the times I feel like I’m more likely to have to go through the darker back areas. And I’ll take the long way around the loading area instead of just walking through it normally, even if it makes no sense time-wise. If I have to check the stairwell, I’ll do it, but I kind of rush through it with my phone in my hand, like I’m ready to turn the flashlight on or call someone, and I’m scared the whole time.\n\nSo outside of work, no, not in a major way. But at work it’s definitely making me avoid things or get through them as fast as possible.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your fear of bats is affecting your ability to enjoy or participate in daily activities outside of your job?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any clear way. Like, day-to-day outside of work, I’m still doing the normal things I need to do. I go to the store, I see people, I can go out to eat or whatever. I’m not sitting at home refusing to leave because of it.\n\nI mean, if I’m outside at night and something makes me think about bats — like if it’s quiet and dark, or I’m walking near trees — then I might feel uncomfortable, and I’ll probably look around more than I need to. But I wouldn’t say it’s stopping me from enjoying my life outside of work in a major way.\n\nIt’s really more that work puts me in situations where I feel like I can’t just avoid it without consequences. Outside of work, it’s more like… if I don’t want to walk through some dark area, I just don’t, and nobody really notices or cares. So I haven’t noticed it affecting my daily activities outside my job too much.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried that your fear of bats might spread to other things or situations, making it harder for you to manage your daily life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve thought about it that way, honestly. Like… “spread” to other things? I guess I worry sometimes that if I keep avoiding the bat-related stuff at work, maybe I’ll get more and more nervous about those areas in general, even if there isn’t actually a bat there. But I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nI haven’t really noticed it turning into a fear of, like, all animals or being outside in general or anything like that. It still feels pretty specifically tied to bats, or places where I imagine one could be hiding. Like dark corners, stairwells, the loading area—those kinds of spots. But I don’t know if I’m afraid of the place itself, or if my brain is just going, “That’s where a bat could be.”\n\nSo… I guess I’m a little worried it could get harder to manage if I let it keep controlling what I do, but I can’t say it has spread in a big way so far. At least not that I’ve clearly noticed.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of bats affected your relationships with family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, my family and friends know I don’t like bats, or at least I think they’ve picked up on it, but it hasn’t turned into arguments or anything like that.\n\nI’m not avoiding seeing them because of it, and I’m not asking people to change plans around me, at least not in any big way. If someone wanted to do something outdoors at night, I might feel a little tense depending on where it was, but I don’t think it’s gotten to the point where it’s affecting the relationship itself.\n\nAt work it feels more obvious because there are certain areas I have to deal with, but with family or friends, I guess it just doesn’t come up that much. So… no, I wouldn’t say it’s really affected my relationships.\n\nDoctor: Are there any parts of your daily routine that you've had to change because of your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not my whole daily routine, but there are definitely parts of it I’ve adjusted, mostly around work.\n\nThe biggest thing is closing shifts. I’ve been trying to swap out of those when I can, or trade with somebody else, because that’s when I get more nervous about running into bats, especially around the back areas or when it’s darker. It’s kind of embarrassing because it adds time and it probably looks unnecessary, but I just feel better not going through that spot if I can avoid it. Yeah… I think it is, more than I wanted to admit at first. I was kind of telling myself it was just an inconvenience, like, “Okay, I don’t like bats, so I avoid a couple areas at work,” but it’s starting to affect something bigger.

My manager has noticed that I keep trying to trade out of closing duties, and that I’m not as dependable when it comes to those after-hours checks. And I get why that looks bad from their side, because if you’re supposed to be a shift lead, you can’t just be unavailable for certain parts of the job. But for me, those are the exact parts that make me feel like I can’t handle it.

The hard part is I’ve been working toward a permanent management position, and now I feel like this could actually cost me that. My manager has started questioning whether I can handle the shift lead role, and that really bothers me because I don’t want to be seen as unreliable. I’ve worked hard to get to that point.

So, yeah, I’d say it’s impacting my quality of life because it’s not just me being uncomfortable anymore. It’s affecting my job and where I might be headed with it, and that’s pretty stressful.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what brings me in is… it’s this reaction I’m having at work, mostly around the stockroom. If I even think there might be a bat in there, or something flapping around, my body just reacts right away. Like, before I can even talk myself through it, my heart kind of jumps, I start sweating, and I get this really strong feeling that I need to get out of there immediately.\n\nIt’s not just “oh, that’s unpleasant” — it feels like my whole body is telling me to leave right then. And I know it probably sounds kind of specific, but it’s been bothering me enough that I figured I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically bats that trigger this reaction, or would other animals or situations cause a similar response?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly bats, yeah. Or… I guess anything that my brain reads as a bat for even half a second. Like if I’m in the back hallway and something flutters quickly, or I catch a dark shape out of the corner of my eye, my mind just jumps straight to “bat,” and then it’s like I’m in danger right then.\n\nThe frustrating thing is I do know, logically, that a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me. I can say that sitting here. But in the moment, it doesn’t feel like that matters. If it looks like one, or sounds like one, or even moves in that quick flapping way, my body and my head react like it’s an actual threat.\n\nDoctor: Does this reaction to bats or things that resemble bats happen only at work, or have you experienced it in other places as well?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I mostly notice it at work, since that’s where I’m around the stockroom and the back areas more. That’s the place my mind goes to right away when I think about it.\n\nI mean, I don’t have a lot of examples outside of work that are as clear. It’s not like I’m walking around all day everywhere expecting bats, if that makes sense. But I also don’t know if that’s because it really only happens at work, or because I’m not usually in places where I’d think there could be one. Like at home I’m not usually dealing with dark storage rooms or back hallways, so it just doesn’t come up in the same way.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is, I’ve mainly experienced it at work, and I haven’t really noticed it much in other places.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this reaction to bats or things that resemble bats?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two years now, I think. It started after this one night at work when I was on a receiving shift, and a bat somehow got into the back corridor.\n\nBefore that, I mean, I don’t remember reacting this strongly. I probably wouldn’t have liked seeing one, but it wasn’t like this. That night kind of sticks in my head, because it was back in that corridor area and it was moving around, and ever since then, anything that reminds me of that has set me off. So… yeah, roughly two years.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or on edge in situations that have nothing to do with bats?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in the same way, at least. I mean, everybody gets stressed, right—work can be annoying, money stuff, customers being difficult, normal life things—but I don’t walk around generally feeling on edge for no reason, if that’s what you mean.\n\nThe anxious feeling I’m talking about is pretty tied to the bat thing, or places where I start thinking there could be one. If I’m just at home watching TV or doing errands or something, I’m not usually tense like that. At least I haven’t really noticed it.\n\nI guess sometimes after something reminds me of it, I might stay a little keyed up for a while, but I wouldn’t say I’m anxious all the time or in situations that have nothing to do with bats. It’s more like my brain needs some kind of trigger to go there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or activities because you're afraid of encountering a bat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I probably want to admit, honestly.\n\nAt work especially, I’ve been swapping out closing shifts when I can, because closing is when I end up around the back areas more, and it’s darker and quieter and my mind starts going there. I don’t always say exactly why, either — I’ll just try to trade with somebody or make it sound like a scheduling thing, because it feels embarrassing to say, “I don’t want to be back there because I’m scared there might be a bat.”\n\nAnd if I have to go near the loading area, I’ll take the long way around instead of going the normal way, even if it’s kind of ridiculous and takes extra time. I know that sounds like a small thing, but I’ll actually plan my route through the store around avoiding that part if I can.\n\nThen there are times I can’t avoid it, like if I have to check the stairwell or something. I’ll still do it, but I make myself do it really fast. I’ll have my phone in my hand, like having the flashlight ready or just… I don’t know, like it makes me feel less trapped. But I’m scared the whole time, and I’m basically trying to get in and out as quickly as possible.\n\nDoctor: Has avoiding places where you might encounter bats started to affect your personal life or relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, outside of work, maybe not so much with friends or dating or anything like that, but it’s definitely affecting my life in a bigger way because of work. Work is kind of where everything is tied together for me right now.\n\nMy manager has started noticing that I keep trying to trade out of closing duties, and I think at first he maybe thought it was just normal schedule stuff, but now it’s happened enough that he’s questioning it. And the closing shift is a big deal because if you’re going to be a shift lead, you have to be someone they can count on after hours — like doing the checks, making sure the back areas are handled, that sort of thing.\n\nAnd that’s the part that really bothers me, because I’ve been trying to work toward a more permanent management position. It’s not like I’m just avoiding some little inconvenience. This could actually cost me that opportunity if they decide I’m not dependable enough.\n\nMy manager hasn’t flat-out said, “You’re not getting it,” but I can tell he’s wondering if I can handle it. He’s asked why I’m always switching those shifts, and I don’t really know how to explain it without sounding ridiculous. So I give these vague answers, which probably makes me look worse.\n\nSo, yeah… it’s not just embarrassing anymore. It feels like this thing could mess up something I’ve been working toward.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations outside of work where you feel like your fear of bats is holding you back or causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I’m trying to think. Not really in a big way, I don’t think. I mean, I don’t love being outside at night if something makes me think about bats, like if it’s really dark or there are trees overhead or something, but I can’t say I’ve stopped seeing people or stopped doing normal life stuff because of it.\n\nMost of the real problem is tied to work, because that’s where I feel like I might actually have to go into those back areas or deal with it if one showed up. Outside work, I can usually just… not put myself in that situation, I guess, or it doesn’t come up as much.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s causing major problems in my personal life, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more like an uncomfortable thought here and there, unless something specifically reminds me of it.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any social activities or hobbies outside of work because of your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: Not really social activities or hobbies, no… at least I don’t think so. I mean, I’m not skipping parties or avoiding friends because of bats or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more that the avoiding shows up at work. Like I said, I’ve been swapping out closing shifts when I can, because those are the times I feel like I’m more likely to have to go through the darker back areas. And I’ll take the long way around the loading area instead of just walking through it normally, even if it makes no sense time-wise. If I have to check the stairwell, I’ll do it, but I kind of rush through it with my phone in my hand, like I’m ready to turn the flashlight on or call someone, and I’m scared the whole time.\n\nSo outside of work, no, not in a major way. But at work it’s definitely making me avoid things or get through them as fast as possible.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your fear of bats is affecting your ability to enjoy or participate in daily activities outside of your job?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any clear way. Like, day-to-day outside of work, I’m still doing the normal things I need to do. I go to the store, I see people, I can go out to eat or whatever. I’m not sitting at home refusing to leave because of it.\n\nI mean, if I’m outside at night and something makes me think about bats — like if it’s quiet and dark, or I’m walking near trees — then I might feel uncomfortable, and I’ll probably look around more than I need to. But I wouldn’t say it’s stopping me from enjoying my life outside of work in a major way.\n\nIt’s really more that work puts me in situations where I feel like I can’t just avoid it without consequences. Outside of work, it’s more like… if I don’t want to walk through some dark area, I just don’t, and nobody really notices or cares. So I haven’t noticed it affecting my daily activities outside my job too much.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried that your fear of bats might spread to other things or situations, making it harder for you to manage your daily life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve thought about it that way, honestly. Like… “spread” to other things? I guess I worry sometimes that if I keep avoiding the bat-related stuff at work, maybe I’ll get more and more nervous about those areas in general, even if there isn’t actually a bat there. But I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nI haven’t really noticed it turning into a fear of, like, all animals or being outside in general or anything like that. It still feels pretty specifically tied to bats, or places where I imagine one could be hiding. Like dark corners, stairwells, the loading area—those kinds of spots. But I don’t know if I’m afraid of the place itself, or if my brain is just going, “That’s where a bat could be.”\n\nSo… I guess I’m a little worried it could get harder to manage if I let it keep controlling what I do, but I can’t say it has spread in a big way so far. At least not that I’ve clearly noticed.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of bats affected your relationships with family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, my family and friends know I don’t like bats, or at least I think they’ve picked up on it, but it hasn’t turned into arguments or anything like that.\n\nI’m not avoiding seeing them because of it, and I’m not asking people to change plans around me, at least not in any big way. If someone wanted to do something outdoors at night, I might feel a little tense depending on where it was, but I don’t think it’s gotten to the point where it’s affecting the relationship itself.\n\nAt work it feels more obvious because there are certain areas I have to deal with, but with family or friends, I guess it just doesn’t come up that much. So… no, I wouldn’t say it’s really affected my relationships.\n\nDoctor: Are there any parts of your daily routine that you've had to change because of your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not my whole daily routine, but there are definitely parts of it I’ve adjusted, mostly around work.\n\nThe biggest thing is closing shifts. I’ve been trying to swap out of those when I can, or trade with somebody else, because that’s when I get more nervous about running into bats, especially around the back areas or when it’s darker. I don’t always say the full reason, honestly. Sometimes I’ll just say I have something going on or ask if someone wants to switch, but in my head it’s really because I don’t want to be there at that time.\n\nAnd then at work I’ll take the long way around the loading area instead of going through it directly. It’s kind of embarrassing because it adds time and it probably looks unnecessary, but I just feel better not going through that spot if I can avoid it. If I have to pass by it, I’m kind of tense the whole time.\n\nThe stairwell is another one. If I have to check it, I’ll still do it, but I do it really fast. I’ll hold my phone in my hand, like that somehow makes me feel more prepared, and I’ll kind of shine the light or keep it ready. I know it probably looks ridiculous, but I’m scared the whole time, and I’m just trying to get in and out as quickly as possible.\n\nSo, yeah, I guess I have changed some things. It’s not like every minute of my day is different, but I’m definitely planning around it more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your fear of bats is significantly impacting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, more than I wanted to admit at first. I was kind of telling myself it was just an inconvenience, like, “Okay, I don’t like bats, so I avoid a couple areas at work,” but it’s starting to affect something bigger.\n\nMy manager has noticed that I keep trying to trade out of closing duties, and that I’m not as dependable when it comes to those after-hours checks. And I get why that looks bad from their side, because if you’re supposed to be a shift lead, you can’t just be unavailable for certain parts of the job. But for me, those are the exact parts that make me feel like I can’t handle it.\n\nThe hard part is I’ve been working toward a permanent management position, and now I feel like this could actually cost me that. My manager has started questioning whether I can handle the shift lead role, and that really bothers me because I don’t want to be seen as unreliable. I’ve worked hard to get to that point.\n\nSo, yeah, I’d say it’s impacting my quality of life because it’s not just me being uncomfortable anymore. It’s affecting my job and where I might be headed with it, and that’s pretty stressful.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

And I know it probably sounds kind of specific, but it’s been bothering me enough that I figured I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically bats that trigger this reaction, or would other animals or situations cause a similar response?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly bats, yeah. So… no, I wouldn’t say it’s really affected my relationships.\n\nDoctor: Are there any parts of your daily routine that you've had to change because of your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not my whole daily routine, but there are definitely parts of it I’ve adjusted, mostly around work.\n\nThe biggest thing is closing shifts. It’s kind of embarrassing because it adds time and it probably looks unnecessary, but I just feel better not going through that spot if I can avoid it.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think so. Not really with friends or family.

Like, if someone invites me to dinner or to come over, I’m not avoiding that because of bats. I still see people, I still talk to them, I still go to normal stuff. It’s not like I’m isolating myself or canceling plans all the time because of it.

I guess if there was something very specific, like someone wanted to go walking around outside at night in a wooded area or something, I might feel uneasy and maybe try to steer it toward something else. But that honestly doesn’t come up much in my social life. Most of the problem is more tied to work situations, not family gatherings or hanging out with friends.

So, no… I wouldn’t say it’s causing me to avoid social interactions or events with them.
